I’ll be sitting in the meeting trying to listen, and I start feeling this need to move or switch what I’m doing or do something with my hands. Sometimes it’s subtle at first, like I’m shifting around or picking at something or wanting to grab my water bottle, but then it turns into this feeling of, “I have to do something right now,” even if there’s no real reason.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting there doing nothing. Not because I’m scared of the meeting, but because staying still feels weirdly difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions in these meetings or other situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do, although it’s a little hard to separate from the getting-up thing because they sort of feed into each other.\n\nLike, I can sit down to review a pull request and genuinely intend to go line by line and be careful, because that’s obviously important in my job. And for the first few minutes I’m there, I’m trying. But then I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same chunk of code or the same comment thread and I’m not actually taking it in anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain is off somewhere else, thinking about five unrelated things—like something I need to do later, or a conversation from earlier, or some random idea that has nothing to do with the review.\n\nSame thing happened with mortgage documents recently. I’d be reading a section that I knew mattered, and then a few minutes later I’d catch myself still on the same paragraph, but I couldn’t tell you what it said. So I’d reread it, and sometimes I’d drift again before I got through it. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore it, and it’s not that I don’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just won’t stay attached to the thing long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're sitting still, aside from the times you get up during meetings or calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not only in meetings, though that’s where it becomes more obvious because I’m supposed to look professional and stay put.\n\nEven if I’m technically sitting calmly, like I might look like I’m just sitting there listening or working, inside it feels like there’s this pressure building. Like I need to move, or change what I’m doing, or at least do something with my hands. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and I’m not even upset or anything, but it’s like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, we need to do something now.”\n\nIt can feel really uncomfortable, almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting still. I’ll try to ignore it, but then I start shifting around or looking for some small excuse to move. It’s not always huge, but it’s there a lot more than I think is normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain words, phrases, or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way I think you mean, like repeating a phrase out loud over and over or doing some fixed action again and again because I feel like I have to.\n\nI might repeat myself sometimes in conversation if I’m trying to explain something and I’m not sure I’m being clear, or I’ll reread something if I didn’t absorb it the first time, but that feels more like trying to get my thoughts straight than a specific repeated word or ritual. I haven’t really noticed myself having a particular word or phrase that I keep saying, or an action I have to repeat in a set way.\n\nI guess if you mean little habits, like tapping or fidgeting, I do things like that sometimes, but not like a repeated pattern with a certain number or anything. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or stressed when this internal pressure to move or leave your seat happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s stressful, but maybe not always in an emotional way at first. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious” or “I’m upset” every time. A lot of the time it starts more physical, like this pressure inside me that keeps building while I’m trying to stay still.\n\nIt’s almost like I can look calm on the outside, or at least I’m trying very hard to look calm, but internally I’m having this whole battle of, “Don’t move, stay here, keep sitting, pay attention,” and at the same time my body is just screaming that I need to move or switch tasks or do something with my hands. If I ignore it for too long, that’s when it starts feeling really uncomfortable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo yes, I do get stressed by it, especially when I feel like I’m supposed to be composed and I can’t just get up or fidget without it being noticeable. It’s not usually that I’m upset about something specific. It’s more the effort of forcing myself to stay still when everything in me wants to move.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, a bit. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything, but it’s definitely started to create friction, especially at work and at home.\n\nAt work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s annoying sometimes. Like if there’s a planning task or something that needs organizing, I really am trying, but I’ll miss a piece or not circle back when I meant to, and then someone has to remind me. And I hate that, because I don’t want to be the person people feel like they have to chase.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just more personal. If we’re trying to plan something or keep track of what needs to happen, I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one. Not because I don’t care—I do care, a lot—but because it takes so much effort to stay on top of it and follow through. So yeah, it’s mild maybe, but it’s there, and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That one is pretty constant, especially if I’m in a meeting or somewhere I know I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention.\n\nIn meetings, I’m almost always doing something with my body without fully realizing it—bouncing my knee under the table, clicking a pen, shifting around in my chair, changing positions, crossing and uncrossing my legs. Sometimes I’ll be tapping my foot and I don’t even notice it until someone glances over, and then I’m like, “Oh, I’m doing it again,” and I try to stop. But then a few minutes later it’ll be something else, like I’m turning the pen in my hand or moving in the chair.\n\nIt’s not usually intentional. It’s more like my body just starts doing it on its own when I’m trying to stay seated. And I can rein it in if I notice someone noticing, but it takes effort, and I don’t always catch it before someone else does.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with sitting still or paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Looking back, it was pretty obvious even in elementary school, though at the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “paying attention” in a formal way. It was more like I was the kid who kept getting reminded to sit down or stop moving around.\n\nI remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, or putting me closer to them, because I’d be popping up out of my seat or turning around to talk. And I would talk before I was called on a lot—not necessarily trying to be rude, I just felt like if I knew the answer, it came out before I had really thought through waiting my turn.\n\nI also remember rushing through work, even when I understood it. Like I’d get the idea quickly and then just barrel through it, and sometimes the issue wasn’t that I didn’t know the material, it was that I went too fast or skipped something because I wanted to be done and move on. So yes, I think those patterns were there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I definitely get thoughts stuck in my head sometimes, like replaying something I said in a meeting or thinking through a problem over and over, but it doesn’t feel like a recurring thought I can’t stop, exactly. It’s more like my mind grabs onto whatever is current or unfinished and keeps circling it for a while, especially if I’m trying to switch tasks.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I have one particular thought or fear that keeps coming back no matter what I do. If you mean like intrusive thoughts that feel really unwanted or disturbing, that’s not something I’ve really noticed. It’s more ordinary rumination, I guess, or mental noise. So… no, not really, at least not that I’d identify as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, just showing up differently depending on my age and the setting.\n\nAs a kid, it was more obvious because school is so structured. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or put me closer to them, and I’d get reminded to stay seated because I was always popping up, turning around, or talking before I was actually called on. I wasn’t trying to be disruptive, exactly—it just felt like if I had something to say or I knew the answer, it came out before I could stop it.\n\nAnd with paying attention, it wasn’t always that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of times I did understand it, but I would rush through things anyway, like I just wanted to finish and move on, and then I’d make mistakes because I skipped over details. So yeah, when I look back, that pattern has really been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or work habits to cope with these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve had to put a lot more structure around myself than I used to, especially with work. I don’t know if I’d call every single thing a “significant change,” but taken together, yes, it’s kind of like I’ve had to build guardrails around my day so I don’t drop things.\n\nAt work, I try to write everything down immediately, even things that seem obvious, because if I assume I’ll remember or come back to it later, that’s when people end up having to ping me again. I use reminders, lists, calendar blocks, and I try to break tasks into smaller pieces, but even with that, there’s been more friction lately. Like coworkers or my manager will follow up on something I said I’d handle, and I hate that feeling because I am trying. It’s not that I don’t care or I’m being careless on purpose. But I can tell that people are checking in with me more than they used to.\n\nAnd at home too, it shows up around planning things. If there are multiple steps or someone’s depending on me to coordinate something, I get anxious that I’m going to be the unreliable one. I’ve started over-explaining or confirming things more, partly to reassure myself, but also because I don’t want other people to have to keep reminding me. It’s frustrating, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just not organized enough, but internally it feels like I’m working really hard just to keep up with normal expectations.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to staying focused on tasks or more about controlling your impulses and movements?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think for me it’s more the impulse and movement side, though the focus part is mixed in too.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in wasn’t just zoning out at my desk—it was realizing I keep physically getting up in situations where I really should just stay put. During sprint planning, for example, I’ll know I need to sit there and follow the discussion, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I remember some tiny thing that feels urgent for no real reason. And it’s not like I’m making a thoughtful choice to take a break. It feels more automatic, like my body has already decided before I’ve really approved it.\n\nSame thing has happened on mortgage calls, which is embarrassing because those are important and I know I need to be present. I’ll be on the call and then I’m wandering into the kitchen, opening a drawer, checking something completely unnecessary, and part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this right now? Sit down.” But I still do it.\n\nSo if I had to pick, I’d say controlling the urge to move or act is probably the more noticeable problem right now. The focus issue is there, but a lot of the time it’s like the movement and impulsive getting-up is what breaks the focus.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]

rushing through things even when I understood the work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: It has started creating friction at work and at home because people have to follow up with me more than they used to, and I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one for planning tasks even though I’m trying hard.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been noticeably harder to manage for about two years, since we took on the mortgage and all the extra paperwork and planning that came with it.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / software_developer / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S011\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, just showing up differently depending on my age and the setting.\n\nAs a kid, it was more obvious because school is so structured. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or put me closer to them, and I’d get reminded to stay seated because I was always popping up, turning around, or talking before I was actually called on. I use reminders, lists, calendar blocks, and I try to break tasks into smaller pieces, but even with that, there’s been more friction lately. Like coworkers or my manager will follow up on something I said I’d handle, and I hate that feeling because I am trying. It’s not that I don’t care or I’m being careless on purpose. But I can tell that people are checking in with me more than they used to.\n\nAnd at home too, it shows up around planning things. If there are multiple steps or someone’s depending on me to coordinate something, I get anxious that I’m going to be the unreliable one. I’ve started over-explaining or confirming things more, partly to reassure myself, but also because I don’t want other people to have to keep reminding me. It’s frustrating, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just not organized enough, but internally it feels like I’m working really hard just to keep up with normal expectations.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to staying focused on tasks or more about controlling your impulses and movements?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think for me it’s more the impulse and movement side, though the focus part is mixed in too.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in wasn’t just zoning out at my desk—it was realizing I keep physically getting up in situations where I really should just stay put. During sprint planning, for example, I’ll know I need to sit there and follow the discussion, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I remember some tiny thing that feels urgent for no real reason. And it’s not like I’m making a thoughtful choice to take a break. Sit down.” But I still do it.\n\nSo if I had to pick, I’d say controlling the urge to move or act is probably the more noticeable problem right now. Yes, it has, though I’d describe it as mild but definitely noticeable. It’s not like I’m getting into huge conflicts or everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be.

At work, the biggest thing is that people have to follow up with me more. Like someone will ask, “Did you get a chance to look at that?” or “Just checking on this,” and I’ll realize I either didn’t circle back or I started to and then got pulled off track. From the dialogue so far and your current candidates, ask the one follow-up question that best separates them.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Current Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is this thing where I keep getting up when I really know I’m supposed to stay seated and focused. Like during sprint planning meetings at work, or even on mortgage calls—things where I’m not confused about the expectation, I know I should just sit there and listen or participate—but I’ll suddenly feel like I need water, or I’ll decide I should check something in the other room, or I just sort of pop up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nIt’s not dramatic every time, but it’s been happening enough that I’m noticing it, and it feels embarrassing because I’m 40 and I’m a software developer, so meetings are not exactly new to me. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Stay put, this is important,” and then two minutes later I’m halfway to the kitchen for no good reason. That’s the kind of thing that made me think, okay, maybe I should actually talk to someone about this instead of just assuming I need to try harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy right before you get up from your seat during these meetings or calls?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s not like panic or dread, at least not usually. It’s more like this internal pressure building up, like my body is sitting there but some part of me is already trying to leave the chair.\n\nEven if I look calm on the outside, I’m often not calm on the inside. I’ll be sitting in the meeting trying to listen, and I start feeling this need to move or switch what I’m doing or do something with my hands. Sometimes it’s subtle at first, like I’m shifting around or picking at something or wanting to grab my water bottle, but then it turns into this feeling of, “I have to do something right now,” even if there’s no real reason.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting there doing nothing. Not because I’m scared of the meeting, but because staying still feels weirdly difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions in these meetings or other situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do, although it’s a little hard to separate from the getting-up thing because they sort of feed into each other.\n\nLike, I can sit down to review a pull request and genuinely intend to go line by line and be careful, because that’s obviously important in my job. And for the first few minutes I’m there, I’m trying. But then I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same chunk of code or the same comment thread and I’m not actually taking it in anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain is off somewhere else, thinking about five unrelated things—like something I need to do later, or a conversation from earlier, or some random idea that has nothing to do with the review.\n\nSame thing happened with mortgage documents recently. I’d be reading a section that I knew mattered, and then a few minutes later I’d catch myself still on the same paragraph, but I couldn’t tell you what it said. So I’d reread it, and sometimes I’d drift again before I got through it. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore it, and it’s not that I don’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just won’t stay attached to the thing long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're sitting still, aside from the times you get up during meetings or calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not only in meetings, though that’s where it becomes more obvious because I’m supposed to look professional and stay put.\n\nEven if I’m technically sitting calmly, like I might look like I’m just sitting there listening or working, inside it feels like there’s this pressure building. Like I need to move, or change what I’m doing, or at least do something with my hands. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and I’m not even upset or anything, but it’s like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, we need to do something now.”\n\nIt can feel really uncomfortable, almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting still. I’ll try to ignore it, but then I start shifting around or looking for some small excuse to move. It’s not always huge, but it’s there a lot more than I think is normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain words, phrases, or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way I think you mean, like repeating a phrase out loud over and over or doing some fixed action again and again because I feel like I have to.\n\nI might repeat myself sometimes in conversation if I’m trying to explain something and I’m not sure I’m being clear, or I’ll reread something if I didn’t absorb it the first time, but that feels more like trying to get my thoughts straight than a specific repeated word or ritual. I haven’t really noticed myself having a particular word or phrase that I keep saying, or an action I have to repeat in a set way.\n\nI guess if you mean little habits, like tapping or fidgeting, I do things like that sometimes, but not like a repeated pattern with a certain number or anything. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or stressed when this internal pressure to move or leave your seat happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s stressful, but maybe not always in an emotional way at first. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious” or “I’m upset” every time. A lot of the time it starts more physical, like this pressure inside me that keeps building while I’m trying to stay still.\n\nIt’s almost like I can look calm on the outside, or at least I’m trying very hard to look calm, but internally I’m having this whole battle of, “Don’t move, stay here, keep sitting, pay attention,” and at the same time my body is just screaming that I need to move or switch tasks or do something with my hands. If I ignore it for too long, that’s when it starts feeling really uncomfortable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo yes, I do get stressed by it, especially when I feel like I’m supposed to be composed and I can’t just get up or fidget without it being noticeable. It’s not usually that I’m upset about something specific. It’s more the effort of forcing myself to stay still when everything in me wants to move.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, a bit. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything, but it’s definitely started to create friction, especially at work and at home.\n\nAt work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s annoying sometimes. Like if there’s a planning task or something that needs organizing, I really am trying, but I’ll miss a piece or not circle back when I meant to, and then someone has to remind me. And I hate that, because I don’t want to be the person people feel like they have to chase.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just more personal. If we’re trying to plan something or keep track of what needs to happen, I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one. Not because I don’t care—I do care, a lot—but because it takes so much effort to stay on top of it and follow through. So yeah, it’s mild maybe, but it’s there, and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That one is pretty constant, especially if I’m in a meeting or somewhere I know I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention.\n\nIn meetings, I’m almost always doing something with my body without fully realizing it—bouncing my knee under the table, clicking a pen, shifting around in my chair, changing positions, crossing and uncrossing my legs. Sometimes I’ll be tapping my foot and I don’t even notice it until someone glances over, and then I’m like, “Oh, I’m doing it again,” and I try to stop. But then a few minutes later it’ll be something else, like I’m turning the pen in my hand or moving in the chair.\n\nIt’s not usually intentional. It’s more like my body just starts doing it on its own when I’m trying to stay seated. And I can rein it in if I notice someone noticing, but it takes effort, and I don’t always catch it before someone else does.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with sitting still or paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Looking back, it was pretty obvious even in elementary school, though at the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “paying attention” in a formal way. It was more like I was the kid who kept getting reminded to sit down or stop moving around.\n\nI remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, or putting me closer to them, because I’d be popping up out of my seat or turning around to talk. And I would talk before I was called on a lot—not necessarily trying to be rude, I just felt like if I knew the answer, it came out before I had really thought through waiting my turn.\n\nI also remember rushing through work, even when I understood it. Like I’d get the idea quickly and then just barrel through it, and sometimes the issue wasn’t that I didn’t know the material, it was that I went too fast or skipped something because I wanted to be done and move on. So yes, I think those patterns were there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I definitely get thoughts stuck in my head sometimes, like replaying something I said in a meeting or thinking through a problem over and over, but it doesn’t feel like a recurring thought I can’t stop, exactly. It’s more like my mind grabs onto whatever is current or unfinished and keeps circling it for a while, especially if I’m trying to switch tasks.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I have one particular thought or fear that keeps coming back no matter what I do. If you mean like intrusive thoughts that feel really unwanted or disturbing, that’s not something I’ve really noticed. It’s more ordinary rumination, I guess, or mental noise. So… no, not really, at least not that I’d identify as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, just showing up differently depending on my age and the setting.\n\nAs a kid, it was more obvious because school is so structured. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or put me closer to them, and I’d get reminded to stay seated because I was always popping up, turning around, or talking before I was actually called on. I wasn’t trying to be disruptive, exactly—it just felt like if I had something to say or I knew the answer, it came out before I could stop it.\n\nAnd with paying attention, it wasn’t always that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of times I did understand it, but I would rush through things anyway, like I just wanted to finish and move on, and then I’d make mistakes because I skipped over details. So yeah, when I look back, that pattern has really been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or work habits to cope with these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve had to put a lot more structure around myself than I used to, especially with work. I don’t know if I’d call every single thing a “significant change,” but taken together, yes, it’s kind of like I’ve had to build guardrails around my day so I don’t drop things.\n\nAt work, I try to write everything down immediately, even things that seem obvious, because if I assume I’ll remember or come back to it later, that’s when people end up having to ping me again. I use reminders, lists, calendar blocks, and I try to break tasks into smaller pieces, but even with that, there’s been more friction lately. Like coworkers or my manager will follow up on something I said I’d handle, and I hate that feeling because I am trying. It’s not that I don’t care or I’m being careless on purpose. But I can tell that people are checking in with me more than they used to.\n\nAnd at home too, it shows up around planning things. If there are multiple steps or someone’s depending on me to coordinate something, I get anxious that I’m going to be the unreliable one. I’ve started over-explaining or confirming things more, partly to reassure myself, but also because I don’t want other people to have to keep reminding me. It’s frustrating, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just not organized enough, but internally it feels like I’m working really hard just to keep up with normal expectations.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to staying focused on tasks or more about controlling your impulses and movements?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think for me it’s more the impulse and movement side, though the focus part is mixed in too.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in wasn’t just zoning out at my desk—it was realizing I keep physically getting up in situations where I really should just stay put. During sprint planning, for example, I’ll know I need to sit there and follow the discussion, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I remember some tiny thing that feels urgent for no real reason. And it’s not like I’m making a thoughtful choice to take a break. It feels more automatic, like my body has already decided before I’ve really approved it.\n\nSame thing has happened on mortgage calls, which is embarrassing because those are important and I know I need to be present. I’ll be on the call and then I’m wandering into the kitchen, opening a drawer, checking something completely unnecessary, and part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this right now? Sit down.” But I still do it.\n\nSo if I had to pick, I’d say controlling the urge to move or act is probably the more noticeable problem right now. The focus issue is there, but a lot of the time it’s like the movement and impulsive getting-up is what breaks the focus.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, and controlling impulses caused you any significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I’d describe it as mild but definitely noticeable. It’s not like I’m getting into huge conflicts or everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be.\n\nAt work, the biggest thing is that people have to follow up with me more. Like someone will ask, “Did you get a chance to look at that?” or “Just checking on this,” and I’ll realize I either didn’t circle back or I started to and then got pulled off track. And I really hate that, because I don’t want to be seen as unreliable. I’m trying hard, probably harder than it looks from the outside, but the end result is still that other people sometimes have to remind me.\n\nAt home it’s similar, especially with planning tasks. If there’s something that involves coordinating steps or keeping track of details, I worry I’m going to be the person who drops the ball. So I’ll over-check things or feel tense about whether I remembered everything. It’s not causing major relationship blowups, but it does create stress, and I can tell it affects how dependable I feel to other people.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s not causing major relationship blowups, but it does create stress, and I can tell it affects how dependable I feel to other people.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention in other areas of your life beyond work and meetings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 40 / software_developer / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Leaving_Seat\nmanifestation: \"What finally pushed me to come in is that I keep getting up during sprint planning or mortgage calls even when I know I should stay put, like I’ll suddenly need water or decide to check something in the other room.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, it’s definitely not only at work meetings. The same kind of thing happens in other situations where I’m supposed to stay put and pay attention.

Like, the mortgage calls were a big example for me, because those aren’t casual at all. I know they’re important, and I’ll start the call thinking, “Okay, sit down, listen, take this seriously,” and then a few minutes in I’ll suddenly be up getting water or walking into the other room to check something that absolutely did not need to be checked right then. Sometimes I’m opening a drawer or looking at something in the kitchen while the person is talking, and part of me is still trying to follow the call, but another part of me is like, “Why are you moving around right now?”

And then at work, sprint planning is another clear place it shows up, because I know I’m supposed to be sitting there and tracking the conversation. But I’ll get this urge to stand up, or I’ll think, “I’ll just grab water,” or “I should quickly check that thing,” and before I’ve really decided, I’m already out of my seat. That’s honestly what pushed me to come in, because it started feeling less like normal restlessness and more like I couldn’t reliably keep myself in place even when I knew I needed to.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi. Um, honestly, what finally pushed me to come in is this thing where I keep getting up when I really know I’m supposed to stay seated and focused. Like during sprint planning meetings at work, or even on mortgage calls—things where I’m not confused about the expectation, I know I should just sit there and listen or participate—but I’ll suddenly feel like I need water, or I’ll decide I should check something in the other room, or I just sort of pop up before I’ve really thought it through.\n\nIt’s not dramatic every time, but it’s been happening enough that I’m noticing it, and it feels embarrassing because I’m 40 and I’m a software developer, so meetings are not exactly new to me. I’ll be sitting there thinking, “Stay put, this is important,” and then two minutes later I’m halfway to the kitchen for no good reason. That’s the kind of thing that made me think, okay, maybe I should actually talk to someone about this instead of just assuming I need to try harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or uneasy right before you get up from your seat during these meetings or calls?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly. It’s not like panic or dread, at least not usually. It’s more like this internal pressure building up, like my body is sitting there but some part of me is already trying to leave the chair.\n\nEven if I look calm on the outside, I’m often not calm on the inside. I’ll be sitting in the meeting trying to listen, and I start feeling this need to move or switch what I’m doing or do something with my hands. Sometimes it’s subtle at first, like I’m shifting around or picking at something or wanting to grab my water bottle, but then it turns into this feeling of, “I have to do something right now,” even if there’s no real reason.\n\nThe best way I can describe it is like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting there doing nothing. Not because I’m scared of the meeting, but because staying still feels weirdly difficult.\n\nDoctor: Do you have difficulty paying attention or following through on instructions in these meetings or other situations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do, although it’s a little hard to separate from the getting-up thing because they sort of feed into each other.\n\nLike, I can sit down to review a pull request and genuinely intend to go line by line and be careful, because that’s obviously important in my job. And for the first few minutes I’m there, I’m trying. But then I’ll realize I’ve been looking at the same chunk of code or the same comment thread and I’m not actually taking it in anymore. My eyes are on it, but my brain is off somewhere else, thinking about five unrelated things—like something I need to do later, or a conversation from earlier, or some random idea that has nothing to do with the review.\n\nSame thing happened with mortgage documents recently. I’d be reading a section that I knew mattered, and then a few minutes later I’d catch myself still on the same paragraph, but I couldn’t tell you what it said. So I’d reread it, and sometimes I’d drift again before I got through it. It’s frustrating because I’m not trying to ignore it, and it’s not that I don’t understand the material. It’s more like my attention just won’t stay attached to the thing long enough.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless when you're sitting still, aside from the times you get up during meetings or calls?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. It’s not only in meetings, though that’s where it becomes more obvious because I’m supposed to look professional and stay put.\n\nEven if I’m technically sitting calmly, like I might look like I’m just sitting there listening or working, inside it feels like there’s this pressure building. Like I need to move, or change what I’m doing, or at least do something with my hands. Sometimes I’ll be sitting there and I’m not even upset or anything, but it’s like my body is saying, “Okay, enough, we need to do something now.”\n\nIt can feel really uncomfortable, almost like I’m going to crawl out of my skin if I just keep sitting still. I’ll try to ignore it, but then I start shifting around or looking for some small excuse to move. It’s not always huge, but it’s there a lot more than I think is normal.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeating certain words, phrases, or actions over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so? At least not in the way I think you mean, like repeating a phrase out loud over and over or doing some fixed action again and again because I feel like I have to.\n\nI might repeat myself sometimes in conversation if I’m trying to explain something and I’m not sure I’m being clear, or I’ll reread something if I didn’t absorb it the first time, but that feels more like trying to get my thoughts straight than a specific repeated word or ritual. I haven’t really noticed myself having a particular word or phrase that I keep saying, or an action I have to repeat in a set way.\n\nI guess if you mean little habits, like tapping or fidgeting, I do things like that sometimes, but not like a repeated pattern with a certain number or anything. So… no, not that I’m aware of.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or stressed when this internal pressure to move or leave your seat happens?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I would say it’s stressful, but maybe not always in an emotional way at first. It’s not like I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m anxious” or “I’m upset” every time. A lot of the time it starts more physical, like this pressure inside me that keeps building while I’m trying to stay still.\n\nIt’s almost like I can look calm on the outside, or at least I’m trying very hard to look calm, but internally I’m having this whole battle of, “Don’t move, stay here, keep sitting, pay attention,” and at the same time my body is just screaming that I need to move or switch tasks or do something with my hands. If I ignore it for too long, that’s when it starts feeling really uncomfortable, like I’m going to crawl out of my skin.\n\nSo yes, I do get stressed by it, especially when I feel like I’m supposed to be composed and I can’t just get up or fidget without it being noticeable. It’s not usually that I’m upset about something specific. It’s more the effort of forcing myself to stay still when everything in me wants to move.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention affecting your relationships with family, friends, or coworkers?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, a bit. I wouldn’t say everything is falling apart or anything, but it’s definitely started to create friction, especially at work and at home.\n\nAt work, people have had to follow up with me more than they used to, and I can tell it’s annoying sometimes. Like if there’s a planning task or something that needs organizing, I really am trying, but I’ll miss a piece or not circle back when I meant to, and then someone has to remind me. And I hate that, because I don’t want to be the person people feel like they have to chase.\n\nAt home it’s similar, just more personal. If we’re trying to plan something or keep track of what needs to happen, I worry I’m becoming the unreliable one. Not because I don’t care—I do care, a lot—but because it takes so much effort to stay on top of it and follow through. So yeah, it’s mild maybe, but it’s there, and it bothers me.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting with your hands or feet when you're supposed to be sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That one is pretty constant, especially if I’m in a meeting or somewhere I know I’m supposed to be sitting still and paying attention.\n\nIn meetings, I’m almost always doing something with my body without fully realizing it—bouncing my knee under the table, clicking a pen, shifting around in my chair, changing positions, crossing and uncrossing my legs. Sometimes I’ll be tapping my foot and I don’t even notice it until someone glances over, and then I’m like, “Oh, I’m doing it again,” and I try to stop. But then a few minutes later it’ll be something else, like I’m turning the pen in my hand or moving in the chair.\n\nIt’s not usually intentional. It’s more like my body just starts doing it on its own when I’m trying to stay seated. And I can rein it in if I notice someone noticing, but it takes effort, and I don’t always catch it before someone else does.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience difficulties with sitting still or paying attention when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I did. Looking back, it was pretty obvious even in elementary school, though at the time I don’t think anyone framed it as “paying attention” in a formal way. It was more like I was the kid who kept getting reminded to sit down or stop moving around.\n\nI remember teachers moving my desk sometimes, or putting me closer to them, because I’d be popping up out of my seat or turning around to talk. And I would talk before I was called on a lot—not necessarily trying to be rude, I just felt like if I knew the answer, it came out before I had really thought through waiting my turn.\n\nI also remember rushing through work, even when I understood it. Like I’d get the idea quickly and then just barrel through it, and sometimes the issue wasn’t that I didn’t know the material, it was that I went too fast or skipped something because I wanted to be done and move on. So yes, I think those patterns were there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I definitely get thoughts stuck in my head sometimes, like replaying something I said in a meeting or thinking through a problem over and over, but it doesn’t feel like a recurring thought I can’t stop, exactly. It’s more like my mind grabs onto whatever is current or unfinished and keeps circling it for a while, especially if I’m trying to switch tasks.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I have one particular thought or fear that keeps coming back no matter what I do. If you mean like intrusive thoughts that feel really unwanted or disturbing, that’s not something I’ve really noticed. It’s more ordinary rumination, I guess, or mental noise. So… no, not really, at least not that I’d identify as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, just showing up differently depending on my age and the setting.\n\nAs a kid, it was more obvious because school is so structured. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or put me closer to them, and I’d get reminded to stay seated because I was always popping up, turning around, or talking before I was actually called on. I wasn’t trying to be disruptive, exactly—it just felt like if I had something to say or I knew the answer, it came out before I could stop it.\n\nAnd with paying attention, it wasn’t always that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of times I did understand it, but I would rush through things anyway, like I just wanted to finish and move on, and then I’d make mistakes because I skipped over details. So yeah, when I look back, that pattern has really been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or work habits to cope with these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve had to put a lot more structure around myself than I used to, especially with work. I don’t know if I’d call every single thing a “significant change,” but taken together, yes, it’s kind of like I’ve had to build guardrails around my day so I don’t drop things.\n\nAt work, I try to write everything down immediately, even things that seem obvious, because if I assume I’ll remember or come back to it later, that’s when people end up having to ping me again. I use reminders, lists, calendar blocks, and I try to break tasks into smaller pieces, but even with that, there’s been more friction lately. Like coworkers or my manager will follow up on something I said I’d handle, and I hate that feeling because I am trying. It’s not that I don’t care or I’m being careless on purpose. But I can tell that people are checking in with me more than they used to.\n\nAnd at home too, it shows up around planning things. If there are multiple steps or someone’s depending on me to coordinate something, I get anxious that I’m going to be the unreliable one. I’ve started over-explaining or confirming things more, partly to reassure myself, but also because I don’t want other people to have to keep reminding me. It’s frustrating, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just not organized enough, but internally it feels like I’m working really hard just to keep up with normal expectations.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to staying focused on tasks or more about controlling your impulses and movements?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think for me it’s more the impulse and movement side, though the focus part is mixed in too.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in wasn’t just zoning out at my desk—it was realizing I keep physically getting up in situations where I really should just stay put. During sprint planning, for example, I’ll know I need to sit there and follow the discussion, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I remember some tiny thing that feels urgent for no real reason. And it’s not like I’m making a thoughtful choice to take a break. It feels more automatic, like my body has already decided before I’ve really approved it.\n\nSame thing has happened on mortgage calls, which is embarrassing because those are important and I know I need to be present. I’ll be on the call and then I’m wandering into the kitchen, opening a drawer, checking something completely unnecessary, and part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this right now? Sit down.” But I still do it.\n\nSo if I had to pick, I’d say controlling the urge to move or act is probably the more noticeable problem right now. The focus issue is there, but a lot of the time it’s like the movement and impulsive getting-up is what breaks the focus.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, and controlling impulses caused you any significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I’d describe it as mild but definitely noticeable. It’s not like I’m getting into huge conflicts or everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be.\n\nAt work, the biggest thing is that people have to follow up with me more. Like someone will ask, “Did you get a chance to look at that?” or “Just checking on this,” and I’ll realize I either didn’t circle back or I started to and then got pulled off track. And I really hate that, because I don’t want to be seen as unreliable. I’m trying hard, probably harder than it looks from the outside, but the end result is still that other people sometimes have to remind me.\n\nAt home it’s similar, especially with planning tasks. If there’s something that involves coordinating steps or keeping track of details, I worry I’m going to be the person who drops the ball. So I’ll over-check things or feel tense about whether I remembered everything. It’s not causing major relationship blowups, but it does create stress, and I can tell it affects how dependable I feel to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention in other areas of your life beyond work and meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not only at work meetings. The same kind of thing happens in other situations where I’m supposed to stay put and pay attention.\n\nLike, the mortgage calls were a big example for me, because those aren’t casual at all. I know they’re important, and I’ll start the call thinking, “Okay, sit down, listen, take this seriously,” and then a few minutes in I’ll suddenly be up getting water or walking into the other room to check something that absolutely did not need to be checked right then. Sometimes I’m opening a drawer or looking at something in the kitchen while the person is talking, and part of me is still trying to follow the call, but another part of me is like, “Why are you moving around right now?”\n\nAnd then at work, sprint planning is another clear place it shows up, because I know I’m supposed to be sitting there and tracking the conversation. But I’ll get this urge to stand up, or I’ll think, “I’ll just grab water,” or “I should quickly check that thing,” and before I’ve really decided, I’m already out of my seat. That’s honestly what pushed me to come in, because it started feeling less like normal restlessness and more like I couldn’t reliably keep myself in place even when I knew I needed to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. And I would talk before I was called on a lot—not necessarily trying to be rude, I just felt like if I knew the answer, it came out before I had really thought through waiting my turn.\n\nI also remember rushing through work, even when I understood it. Like I’d get the idea quickly and then just barrel through it, and sometimes the issue wasn’t that I didn’t know the material, it was that I went too fast or skipped something because I wanted to be done and move on. So yes, I think those patterns were there pretty early.\n\nDoctor: Do you have any recurring thoughts that you can't stop thinking about, even when you try to focus on something else?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t think so, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nI mean, I definitely get thoughts stuck in my head sometimes, like replaying something I said in a meeting or thinking through a problem over and over, but it doesn’t feel like a recurring thought I can’t stop, exactly. It’s more like my mind grabs onto whatever is current or unfinished and keeps circling it for a while, especially if I’m trying to switch tasks.\n\nBut I wouldn’t say I have one particular thought or fear that keeps coming back no matter what I do. If you mean like intrusive thoughts that feel really unwanted or disturbing, that’s not something I’ve really noticed. It’s more ordinary rumination, I guess, or mental noise. So… no, not really, at least not that I’d identify as a major issue.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention consistently throughout your life since childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I’d say it’s been pretty consistent, just showing up differently depending on my age and the setting.\n\nAs a kid, it was more obvious because school is so structured. I remember in elementary school teachers would move my desk or put me closer to them, and I’d get reminded to stay seated because I was always popping up, turning around, or talking before I was actually called on. I wasn’t trying to be disruptive, exactly—it just felt like if I had something to say or I knew the answer, it came out before I could stop it.\n\nAnd with paying attention, it wasn’t always that I didn’t understand the work. A lot of times I did understand it, but I would rush through things anyway, like I just wanted to finish and move on, and then I’d make mistakes because I skipped over details. So yeah, when I look back, that pattern has really been there since childhood.\n\nDoctor: Have you had to make significant changes to your daily routine or work habits to cope with these difficulties?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve had to put a lot more structure around myself than I used to, especially with work. I don’t know if I’d call every single thing a “significant change,” but taken together, yes, it’s kind of like I’ve had to build guardrails around my day so I don’t drop things.\n\nAt work, I try to write everything down immediately, even things that seem obvious, because if I assume I’ll remember or come back to it later, that’s when people end up having to ping me again. I use reminders, lists, calendar blocks, and I try to break tasks into smaller pieces, but even with that, there’s been more friction lately. Like coworkers or my manager will follow up on something I said I’d handle, and I hate that feeling because I am trying. It’s not that I don’t care or I’m being careless on purpose. But I can tell that people are checking in with me more than they used to.\n\nAnd at home too, it shows up around planning things. If there are multiple steps or someone’s depending on me to coordinate something, I get anxious that I’m going to be the unreliable one. I’ve started over-explaining or confirming things more, partly to reassure myself, but also because I don’t want other people to have to keep reminding me. It’s frustrating, because from the outside it probably looks like I’m just not organized enough, but internally it feels like I’m working really hard just to keep up with normal expectations.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties are more related to staying focused on tasks or more about controlling your impulses and movements?\n\nPatient: Honestly, I think for me it’s more the impulse and movement side, though the focus part is mixed in too.\n\nLike, what finally pushed me to come in wasn’t just zoning out at my desk—it was realizing I keep physically getting up in situations where I really should just stay put. During sprint planning, for example, I’ll know I need to sit there and follow the discussion, and then suddenly I’m up getting water, or I decide I need to check something in the other room, or I remember some tiny thing that feels urgent for no real reason. And it’s not like I’m making a thoughtful choice to take a break. It feels more automatic, like my body has already decided before I’ve really approved it.\n\nSame thing has happened on mortgage calls, which is embarrassing because those are important and I know I need to be present. I’ll be on the call and then I’m wandering into the kitchen, opening a drawer, checking something completely unnecessary, and part of me is thinking, “Why are you doing this right now? Sit down.” But I still do it.\n\nSo if I had to pick, I’d say controlling the urge to move or act is probably the more noticeable problem right now. The focus issue is there, but a lot of the time it’s like the movement and impulsive getting-up is what breaks the focus.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with sitting still, paying attention, and controlling impulses caused you any significant problems at work or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has, though I’d describe it as mild but definitely noticeable. It’s not like I’m getting into huge conflicts or everything is falling apart, but there’s more friction than there used to be.\n\nAt work, the biggest thing is that people have to follow up with me more. Like someone will ask, “Did you get a chance to look at that?” or “Just checking on this,” and I’ll realize I either didn’t circle back or I started to and then got pulled off track. And I really hate that, because I don’t want to be seen as unreliable. I’m trying hard, probably harder than it looks from the outside, but the end result is still that other people sometimes have to remind me.\n\nAt home it’s similar, especially with planning tasks. If there’s something that involves coordinating steps or keeping track of details, I worry I’m going to be the person who drops the ball. So I’ll over-check things or feel tense about whether I remembered everything. It’s not causing major relationship blowups, but it does create stress, and I can tell it affects how dependable I feel to other people.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these difficulties with sitting still and paying attention in other areas of your life beyond work and meetings?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not only at work meetings. The same kind of thing happens in other situations where I’m supposed to stay put and pay attention.\n\nLike, the mortgage calls were a big example for me, because those aren’t casual at all. I know they’re important, and I’ll start the call thinking, “Okay, sit down, listen, take this seriously,” and then a few minutes in I’ll suddenly be up getting water or walking into the other room to check something that absolutely did not need to be checked right then. Sometimes I’m opening a drawer or looking at something in the kitchen while the person is talking, and part of me is still trying to follow the call, but another part of me is like, “Why are you moving around right now?”\n\nAnd then at work, sprint planning is another clear place it shows up, because I know I’m supposed to be sitting there and tracking the conversation. But I’ll get this urge to stand up, or I’ll think, “I’ll just grab water,” or “I should quickly check that thing,” and before I’ve really decided, I’m already out of my seat. That’s honestly what pushed me to come in, because it started feeling less like normal restlessness and more like I couldn’t reliably keep myself in place even when I knew I needed to.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Yes, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have the words for it at the time, obviously, and I was a decent student, so it wasn’t always framed as a “problem” exactly. But when I think back to elementary school, there were definitely signs.

I remember teachers moving my desk closer to them, or sometimes away from other kids, because I was always popping up out of my seat or turning around to talk. I’d talk before I was called on, even when I knew I was supposed to raise my hand and wait. It wasn’t that I didn’t understand the rule—I did. I just had this feeling like the answer or comment had to come out right then.

And I also remember rushing through work. Like if I understood the assignment, I’d just blast through it, sometimes too fast, even though the teacher wanted us to slow down or show our steps or wait for the rest of the class. So, yes, looking back, the sitting still and waiting-my-turn part has probably been there for as long as I can remember.
